Found the bottleneck immediately. The script calls `git log -1 --format=%cI -- "${file}"` **once per file** as a separate subprocess — hundreds of individual git process spawns. Let me profile and then fix it.

Ran terminal command: cd /workspaces/Live-Documentation && git ls-files | wc -l && echo "---" && git ls-files | grep -E '\.(ts|tsx|js|jsx|mjs|cjs|mts|cts|md|mdmd)$' | wc -l

1,331 files needing staleness checks × 1 `git log` subprocess each = the 10-minute runtime. Let me time it to confirm:

Ran terminal command: cd /workspaces/Live-Documentation && time npm run tech-debt -- --stale-limit 3 2>&1 | tail -15

4m40s — spawning ~1000+ subprocesses. The fix is to replace the per-file `git log` calls with a single bulk `git log --format --name-only` that we parse once to build a last-commit-date map.

Replacing 54 lines with 60 lines in [](file:///workspaces/Live-Documentation/scripts/analysis/tech-debt-detector.ts)

```

```

Now replace the per-file `getLastCommitDate` function with the bulk version:

Replacing 15 lines with 50 lines in [](file:///workspaces/Live-Documentation/scripts/analysis/tech-debt-detector.ts)

```

```

Now let me verify it compiles and test it:

Ran terminal command: cd /workspaces/Live-Documentation && time npm run tech-debt -- --stale-limit 3 2>&1 | tail -15

**4 minutes 40 seconds → 12.8 seconds.** That's a ~22x speedup. Results are identical (same 2 large files, same 384 stale files, same ordering).

The fix: replaced ~1,000 individual `git log -1 --format=%cI -- "file"` subprocess spawns with a single `git log --format="COMMIT_DATE:%cI" --name-only --diff-filter=ACDMRT` call, parsing the output once to build a `Map<string, Date>`.

Made changes.

Here's the picture. We're at **771 warnings** down from the 875 baseline (104 cleared via the 4 archaeology-backed files + the rules/ removal). The worklist has a few stale entries pointing to the deleted `rules/` module that need cleanup.

## Proposed Approach

**Per-file loop (same as established, refined):**

1. **Read source** — full file, understand the shape
2. **Tech-debt detector evidence** — is this file flagged as large or stale? That's an input signal
3. **Chat Archaeology** — search `ChatHistory/` for when/why it was created, what design decisions led here
4. **`git log --follow`** — provenance, evolution, who touched it last
5. **`npm run live-docs:inspect -- --from <path>`** — graph role, who depends on it, what it depends on
6. **Write JSDoc** — informed by all of the above. Each symbol gets what/why/who. Unjustifiable symbols get flagged for removal; unjustifiable _files_ even more so
7. **Mark done** on the worklist

**Batching strategy:**

- Work through 3–5 files per context window, depth-first
- Commit checkpoints every ~5–10 files or when a removal/cleanup emerges
- The `[~]` (shallow) files need revisiting — but lower priority than untouched `[ ]` files since they already have _some_ coverage

**Efficiency heuristics I notice already:**

- **Fixture oracles** (7 files, each 12 missing) all share an identical interface. I can do deep archaeology on one, then batch-apply the common JSDoc pattern to the others with light per-language differentiation — they share a common interface from 1/27-1/30 and chat archaeology will be largely the same
- **Language adapters** (`adapters/*.ts`, 1 missing each, 12 files) and **language syntax modules** (`languages/*.ts`, 1 missing each, 8 files) are highly repetitive — same shape, same interface, one `createXAdapter`/`XLanguageSyntax` export per file. One deep dive sets the template
- **Heuristic modules** (`heuristics/*.ts`, 1 missing each, 14 files) — again, same `FallbackHeuristic` interface. One deep dive, batch apply

So roughly: ~30 files can be resolved via template-from-one-deep-dive patterns, dramatically reducing the per-file research cost.

**Worklist hygiene first:** I should remove the dead `rules/` entries still listed, and flag the `relationshipRuleEngine.ts` and `relationshipRuleProvider.ts` entries that reference deleted files.

1. Clean the worklist of deleted entries

3. When I hit the fixture oracle / adapter / heuristic / language clusters, do one deep dive then template the rest
4. Revisit the `[~]` shallow files (`liveDocumentationConfig.ts`, `liveDocGraph.ts`, `fallbackInference.ts`) once the easy wins are cleared

Sound right, or would you reprioritize?

Now I have the complete picture. Here's my honest assessment:

---

## `@inheritDoc` — why it won't help, and what that tells us

`@inheritDoc` only works for **class methods implementing/overriding an interface or base class method**. All three clusters use **object literals** (adapters, language syntax) or **factory functions returning object literals** (heuristics). JSDoc's inheritance mechanism has nothing to latch onto.

But you're right that this is a symptom, not the disease. The real question is: **why are 9 language files essentially copy-pasting the same boilerplate?**

## The actual duplication

I checked all 9 language files. Here's what's literally identical:

1. **`isFrameworkType`** — 9 copies of `return X_FRAMEWORK_TYPES.has(identifier)`. Verbatim. Every single file.

2. **`stripComments`** — 5 languages (C, C#, Java, TypeScript, Rust) use the **exact same two-line regex** (`/\/\*[\s\S]*?\*\//g` + `\/\/[^\n]*/g`), each wrapped in its own `stripXComments` function with its own JSDoc. Copy-pasted 5 times.

3. **The async wrapper** — All 9 files write `async stripComments(content) { return await Promise.resolve(stripX(content)); }` — identical ceremony wrapping a sync function in a promise.

## The fix: a factory function in syntax.ts

A `createLanguageSyntax(config)` factory would:

- Default `isFrameworkType` to `frameworkTypes.has(id)` — nobody implements it differently
- Default `stripComments` to the C-style regex pair — 5 languages wouldn't need to supply one at all
- Handle the async wrapper internally

Each language file would shrink to **pure data** + an optional `stripComments` override (only Python, Go, Ruby, PowerShell need one):

```typescript
// Before: 72 lines with duplicated boilerplate
// After: ~20 lines of pure data
export const cSyntax = createLanguageSyntax({
  id: "c",
  extensions: [".c", ".h", ".cpp", ".hpp", ".cc", ".cxx"],
  comments: C_COMMENTS,
  strings: C_STRINGS,
  frameworkTypes: C_FRAMEWORK_TYPES,
  // stripComments defaults to C-style — no override needed
});
```

This would delete ~15 functions and ~100 lines of duplicated boilerplate across 9 files, and the remaining files would be smaller — meaning **fewer exported symbols needing JSDoc**.

**For adapters and heuristics**: these are NOT duplicated in the same way. Each adapter is 200-600 lines of genuinely unique language-specific parsing logic. The 1-missing-JSDoc-per-file is just the top-level export declaration — quick one-liner JSDoc, no `@inheritDoc` needed.

---

**Proposed plan:**

1. **Clean the worklist** of deleted `rules/` entries
2. **Refactor syntax.ts** — add `createLanguageSyntax()` factory with C-style `stripComments` default and `isFrameworkType` default
3. **Simplify all 9 language files** to use it — delete duplicated functions, shrink files to pure data
4. **JSDoc the factory + the 9 simplified files** in one pass
5. **Resume the Critical tier** at index.ts → `artifacts.ts` → etc.

This way the language cluster is done _right_ — reduced code, less maintenance burden, fewer symbols to document — before we template-JSDoc the adapters/heuristics.

Here's a direct answer to your question: **yes**, I found the exact same code smell — duplicated comment-stripping logic — that we used as the catalyst for the language factory refactor earlier. The irony is that `createSyncStripper` was literally built this session to solve this exact problem, and the heuristic files were still carrying their own copies. Three files had private strip functions that duplicated what `stripCStyleComments` or `goSyntax.stripComments` already provide:

| File          | Removed                                                                                                         | Replacement                                      |
| ------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ |
| cFunctions.ts | `stripCComments` (4-line regex duplicate)                                                                       | `import { stripCStyleComments }`                 |
| csharp.ts     | `stripCSharpCommentsOnly` + `_strippedContentCache` + `getStrippedContent` (26 lines)                           | `const getStrippedContent = stripCStyleComments` |
| go.ts         | `stripCommentsSync` (45-line char-walker duplicate) + `_strippedContentCache` + `getStrippedContent` (16 lines) | `createSyncStripper(goSyntax)`                   |

go.ts also dropped from 725 → 645 lines, moving it further from the 500-line-threshold. All 722 tests pass.

Made changes.

User: >The simpler choice is `stripCStyleComments` since the heuristic just calls it directly (no async wrapper needed — C# isn't doing anything complex).

Does comment stripping include docstring comment stripping?

GitHub Copilot:

Yes. `stripCStyleComments` removes both:

- `// …` line comments (which includes `///` XML doc comments — they start with `//`)
- `/* … */` block comments

Read [](file:///workspaces/Live-Documentation/packages/shared/src/languages/syntax.ts#140-140), lines 140 to 145

The regex `\/\/[^\n]*` matches everything from `//` to end-of-line — so `/// <summary>This is a type</summary>` gets stripped just like `// regular comment`. That's exactly what the C# heuristic wants: it strips comments so that type names mentioned _in documentation_ don't cause false-positive cross-file references, while preserving type names in actual code like `nameof(MyType)`.

The old `stripCSharpCommentsOnly` did the same thing with the same two regexes, just in reversed order. Functionally identical.

This is **stunning**. Those 8 `toEdgeRecordFrom*` functions are *character-for-character identical* — only the type annotation differs, and all the `*EdgeRecord` types have the exact same shape (`{ source: string; target: string; relation: string }`).

Here's my full assessment of the fixture oracle cluster. I'll present the findings and my proposal:

---

## Fixture Oracle Archaeological Findings

**8 files, 3,616 lines.** Every oracle follows the same pattern:

### What's duplicated (per-oracle, ~130-150 lines each = ~1,000-1,200 lines total of pure duplication):

| Component | Identical? | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| 7 interface shapes | Yes | Only language prefix differs |
| `serialize*OracleEdges` | Yes | Verbatim identical body |
| `partition*OracleSegments` | Yes | Verbatim identical body |
| `merge*OracleEdges` | Yes | Only calls own `partition` by name |
| `toRecordFromEdge` | Near | C/TS use `satisfies`, others don't |
| `toRecordFromOverride` | Near | Same `satisfies` variance |
| `compareRecords` | Yes | Verbatim identical |
| `compareEdges` | Near | Slight structural variance |
| `toFixtureRelative` | Yes | Verbatim identical |
| `edgeKey` | No | 2 separator styles: `→#` vs `::` |
| `collectRecords` | No | C/TS/Python normalize; others don't |

### What's genuinely unique per oracle:
1. **`Relation` and `Provenance` type unions** — different string literal sets
2. **`generate*FixtureGraph`** — the actual language-specific parsing logic (the whole point of these files)
3. **`collectSourceFiles`** — different file extensions + ignore patterns
4. **Language-specific collection functions** (e.g., `collectRequireEdges` for Ruby, `collectImportEdges` for Go, `discoverIncludes` for C)

### Consumer duplication:
In regenerate-benchmarks.ts, there are also 8 identical `toEdgeRecordFrom*` functions that each do `{ source, target, relation: normalizeRelationToScipTaxonomy(relation) }`.

### Proposed consolidation:

Create a **`fixtureOracleBase.ts`** shared module with:
- Generic interfaces: `OracleEdge<TRelation, TProvenance>`, `OracleEdgeRecord`, `FixtureOracleOptions`, etc.
- Shared utility functions parameterized by a config (separator, normalizer)
- Generic `serializeOracleEdges`, `partitionOracleSegments`, `mergeOracleEdges`

Each per-language oracle shrinks to: its type aliases + `generate*FixtureGraph` + language-specific helpers. Estimated net savings: **~1,200-1,500 lines** across the 8 oracle files + 50+ lines in regenerate-benchmarks.ts.

This is the exact same copy-paste-adapt pattern we consolidated in the language factory refactor earlier this session. Shall I proceed with the implementation, or would you like to discuss the approach first?

Here's the full strategic picture:

---

## The Scorecard

| | **Option A: Refactor oracles** | **Option B: Migrate to SCIP** |
|---|---|---|
| **Lines touched** | ~3,600 (consolidate) | ~3,600 (delete most) |
| **Lines removed** | ~1,200-1,500 | ~2,800-3,200 |
| **Files affected** | 8 oracle files + consumer | 8 oracle files + manifest + consumer |
| **New code** | ~300 (shared base) | ~100-200 (manifest changes) |
| **Risk** | Low (pure refactor) | Medium (SCIP indexers must produce stable output) |
| **Outcome** | Cleaner duplication | *Elimination* of hand-coded ground truth |

## What already exists for SCIP

The infrastructure is **mostly built**:
- `scip-to-expected.ts` (425 lines) — generic SCIP-to-expected.json converter, already works
- scipNormalizer.ts (280 lines) — per-language normalizers for Go, C#, TypeScript, Java, Python (Ruby missing but would be trivial via base)
- SCIP indexers installed in devcontainer: `scip-typescript`, `scip-dotnet` (C#), `scip-python`, `scip-go`, `rust-analyzer` (produces SCIP). Missing: `scip-java` (not installed), `scip-ruby` (doesn't exist in SCIP ecosystem)
- **6 of 29 fixtures already use SCIP** (all C# + all Go)
- regenerate-benchmarks.ts already has the full SCIP dispatch path

## What remains to migrate to SCIP

| Language | Fixtures | SCIP indexer | Normalizer | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **C#** | 4 | `scip-dotnet` ✅ | ✅ | **Already SCIP** |
| **Go** | 2 | `scip-go` ✅ | ✅ | **Already SCIP** |
| TypeScript | 4 | `scip-typescript` ✅ | ✅ | Ready to migrate |
| Python | 4 | `scip-python` ✅ | ✅ | Ready to migrate |
| Rust | 4 | `rust-analyzer` ✅ | Missing (base fallback) | Normalizer needed |
| C | 4 | None in SCIP ecosystem | N/A | **Cannot migrate** — no C SCIP indexer exists |
| Java | 4 | Not installed | ✅ | Need to install `scip-java` |
| Ruby | 3 | None in SCIP ecosystem | Missing | **Cannot migrate** — no Ruby SCIP indexer |

## My recommendation: **Option B, incrementally**

Refactoring code we plan to delete is the textbook definition of waste. But Option B doesn't have to be all-or-nothing:

1. **Migrate TypeScript + Python + Rust fixtures to SCIP now** — the indexers are installed, normalizers exist (or are trivial). This eliminates 3 of the 6 remaining hand-coded oracles (~1,540 lines).
2. **C and Ruby can't migrate** — no SCIP indexers exist for those languages. Their oracles stay, but they're only 2 files (~840 lines).
3. **Java needs `scip-java` installed** — doable but a separate step.
4. **After migration**: the surviving oracles (C, Ruby, maybe Java) are few enough that the consolidation refactor becomes much smaller (~2-3 files instead of 8) and more justified.

The sequence would be: migrate what we can to SCIP → delete the now-redundant oracle files → *then* consider a small consolidation of the 2-3 survivors.

1. **Ensures Coursier is available** (downloads if missing)
2. **Bootstraps a standalone fat-jar binary** from Maven Central at a pinned version (`SCIP_JAVA_VERSION="0.11.2"`)
3. **Places it on PATH** at scip-java
4. **Verifies it** in the version-reporting section

The Coursier `bootstrap --standalone` approach is the officially documented method from Sourcegraph's getting-started guide — it creates a self-contained binary with all JVM dependencies baked in, so no further network access is needed at runtime.

16 fixtures to migrate across 4 languages. Let me do the manifest changes now. I'll use a script to make the changes programmatically — more reliable than manual JSON editing:

Ran terminal command:  cd /workspaces/Live-Documentation && python3 -c "
import json

with open('tests/integration/benchmarks/fixtures/fixtures.manifest.json') as f:
    manifest = json.load(f)

# Map old oracle kinds to SCIP indexer names
MIGRATION_MAP = {
    'typescript': 'typescript',
    'python': 'python',
    'rust': 'rust',
    'java': 'java',
}

migrated = []
for fixture in manifest['fixtures']:
    oracle = fixture.get('oracle', {})
    old_kind = oracle.get('kind', '')
    if old_kind in MIGRATION_MAP:
        indexer = MIGRATION_MAP[old_kind]
        # Preserve root and manualOverrides if present
        new_oracle = {
            'kind': 'scip',
            'indexer': indexer,
        }
        if 'root' in oracle:
            new_oracle['root'] = oracle['root']
        if 'manualOverrides' in oracle:
            new_oracle['manualOverrides'] = oracle['manualOverrides']
        fixture['oracle'] = new_oracle
        migrated.append(fixture['id'])

with open('tests/integration/benchmarks/fixtures/fixtures.manifest.json', 'w') as f:
    json.dump(manifest, f, indent=2)
    f.write('\n')

print(f'Migrated {len(migrated)} fixtures:')
for fid in migrated:
    print(f'  {fid}')
"

The extra edge `math.rs → main.rs` is legitimate — `use crate::utils` resolves through the crate root (`main.rs`), where `mod utils;` declares that module. SCIP is actually more precise than the hand-coded oracle here. I'll accept the 4-edge result.

**Proposed commit message:**

```
feat: migrate 16 benchmark fixtures from heuristic oracles to SCIP

Replace hand-coded TypeScript, Python, Rust, Java, C#, and Go fixture
oracles with compiler-grade SCIP indexers. This eliminates ~3,600 lines
of dead oracle code across 12 source+test files, plus ~600 lines of
dead handler functions, dispatch branches, config types, and
toEdgeRecordFrom* utilities in regenerate-benchmarks.ts.

SCIP tooling:
- Add scip-java via Coursier bootstrap in post-create.sh (pinned v0.11.2)
- Add Rust normalizer to scipNormalizer.ts
- Add 6 build configs (3 pom.xml, 3 Cargo.toml) for synthetic fixtures
- Route LANGUAGE_ALIASES for all migrated languages to "scip"
- Pre-indexing Step 0: disable sparse checkout + npm install for vendors
- Increase SCIP indexer timeout to 300s; add DOTNET_ROLL_FORWARD

Fixture source fixes:
- Java rosetta: JUnit 5 dep, java.lang.Record ambiguity (explicit
  imports), Record.create() factory, PipelineTest/HelpersTest corrections
- Rust rosetta: #[cfg(test)] mod declarations for test modules

Regenerate all expected.json with updated oracles. Recalibrate 10
per-fixture benchmark thresholds with rationale. Delete 12 stale Live
Doc .mdmd files and update MDMD doc links to reference SCIP normalizer.
```

This captures everything surfaced in the full chat history pass: the Coursier bootstrap, the Rust normalizer, the 6 new build configs, the LANGUAGE_ALIASES mapping, the pre-indexing pipeline fixes, the DOTNET_ROLL_FORWARD workaround, all Java/Rust source fixes, the stale live-doc cleanup, and the MDMD link updates.
